<details><summary>Logged reasoning block</summary>

```text
Page 25 looks essentially the same - the `height=0.42\textheight,keepaspectratio` didn't help because `keepaspectratio` means the aspect ratio is preserved, so the height won't exceed what the width allows. The figure is already at `\columnwidth`, so the height is determined by the aspect ratio of the PDF file.

Page 36 also looks the same - Table 48 is still not on this page. The `[!t]` didn't pull it here because there's not enough space on this page for the full-width table.

Let me think differently about these issues:


The whitespace on page 25 is structural - the Variance Decomposition section ends partway down the left column, leaving blank space before Feature Distributions continues. The figures themselves are squeezed into the right portion and appear small because `keepaspectratio` is constraining their height. I could try making them full-width with `figure*`, remove the aspect ratio constraint with an explicit height, or accept the gap and pull content from page 26 upward to fill it. The third option seems most promising.

The real culprit is that LaTeX is stretching the left column to balance the two-column layout while reserving space for the figures on the right. I can fix this by adding `\raggedbottom` to stop the stretching, or using `\enlargethispage` to pull more content onto the page.

For page 36, the `table*` placement is the issue - in two-column mode, it defaults to the top of the next page regardless of the placement specifier. Changing from `[!b]` to `[!t]` just shifts where it lands on that following page rather than solving the width problem.

To get Table 48 onto page 36 itself, I could either move the table definition earlier so LaTeX places it at the top of page 36, or convert it from a full-width `table*` to a single-column `table` that fits within one column. Looking at the actual layout, the left column has unused space while the right column is filled with text, so the simplest approach would be to add `\enlargethispage` to squeeze more content onto the current page.

But I'm reconsidering the whole strategy for page 25. Instead of fighting with figure sizes, what if I rearrange the sections so Feature Distributions starts fresh at the top of a page and its figures naturally fill the space, while the shorter Variance Decomposition section follows after? That would preserve the logical flow better than just adding vertical spacing. Though honestly, the user might be more concerned about the visual impression of whitespace than perfect page utilization, so maybe the most effective fix is just to add `\enlargethispage{4\baselineskip}` to page 25 and accept some compromise.

Actually, looking more carefully at what's happening on page 25, the real issue is that LaTeX's `\flushbottom` setting is stretching the vertical glue between sections to fill the page, creating those awkward gaps. If I switch to `\raggedbottom` in the preamble, the whitespace would collapse to the bottom of columns instead, which would look much cleaner overall. I need to trace through the actual section ordering in the document more carefully. The Feature Distributions Analysis section header I'm seeing on page 25 might be a continuation from an earlier page, or there could be multiple instances of this content. Let me check whether the section defined at line 123 is what's appearing on page 25, or if there's something else going on with how the appendix content is being included and wrapped across pages.

Rather than keep debugging the exact line mappings, I should just try adding `\raggedbottom` before the appendix to prevent the vertical stretching on both pages 25 and 36, then verify whether main.tex already has this command or `\flushbottom` set.

    "originalFile": "\\subsection*{DDR Trend Lines}\n\nFigure~\\ref{fig:ddr-trends} shows the per-feature depth trend (mean across all samples at each depth) with individual generator traces overlaid.\nThe consistent downward trajectories for drifting features across all generators confirm that DDR is a universal property of recursive generation.\nShape features exhibit flat trajectories regardless of generator, with near-zero $R^2$ for the linear fit.\n$R^2$ values are computed from ordinary least-squares regression on depth-level means (five points per generator, one per depth 0--4), not per-sample regression; the annotated Spearman $\\rho$ provides the complementary per-sample monotonicity measure.\n\n\\begin{figure}[H]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth,height=0.42\\textheight,keepaspectratio]{fig_appendix_ddr_trends.pdf}\n\\caption{Per-feature depth trends. Bold line: pooled mean ($\\pm$1.96\\,SE shading) across all generators, colored by DDR zone (blue: drifting, wine: intermediate, amber: near-stationary). Thin traces: individual generators. Spearman $\\rho$ and linear $R^2$ are annotated per panel.}\n\\label{fig:ddr-trends}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\subsection*{UMAP Embedding}\n\nFigure~\\ref{fig:umap} shows a 2D UMAP projection of the 7-feature space (7{,}500 stratified samples, StandardScaler normalization, $k$\\,=\\,30 neighbors, min\\_dist\\,=\\,0.3).\nPanel~(a) reveals a depth gradient from gen-0 through gen-4, with progressive overlap at deeper generations consistent with the convergence that motivates the gen-2+ class collapse.\nPanel~(b) shows the 3-class view: gen-0 forms a separable cluster, while gen-1 and gen-2+ overlap substantially, explaining the classification difficulty at this boundary.\n\n\\begin{figure}[H]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth, trim=0 0 356pt 0, clip]{fig_appendix_umap.pdf}\\\\[-2pt]\n{\\small (a) Colored by raw generation depth (0--4)}\\\\[6pt]\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth, trim=356pt 0 0 0, clip]{fig_appendix_umap.pdf}\\\\[-2pt]\n{\\small (b) Colored by 3-class label}\n\\caption{UMAP 2D projection of the 7-feature space ($N$\\,=\\,7{,}500 stratified sample). (a) A gradient structure emerges, with deeper depths converging. (b) Gen-0 separates clearly; gen-1 and gen-2+ overlap, consistent with the high false-negative rate at the gen-1/gen-2+ boundary.}\n\\label{fig:umap}\n\\end{figure}\n",

    "originalFile": "Table~\\ref{tab:iterative-comparison} compares this work with two concurrent studies on iterative LLM generation.\nAll three examine how repeated inference transforms text, but differ in chain operator, analytic lens, and downstream goal.\nOur continuation-chain setting permits richer depth signal and enables the location/shape decomposition that defines DDR.\n\n\\begin{table*}[!t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Comparison with concurrent iterative-generation studies. All three examine how repeated LLM inference transforms text, but differ in chain operator, analytic lens, and downstream goal.}\n\\label{tab:iterative-comparison}\n\\resizebox{\\textwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}l p{2.2cm} p{2.4cm} p{2.4cm} p{2.0cm} c p{3.4cm}@{}}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Study} & \\textbf{Chain Type} & \\textbf{Feature Space} & \\textbf{Analysis Goal} & \\textbf{Generator Scale} & \\textbf{Steps} & \\textbf{Core Insight} \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\citet{broken_telephone}\n  & Back-translation (6 bridge langs); rephrasing (ablation)\n  & Surface overlap (BLEU, ROUGE, METEOR, ChrF, BERTScore) + atomic factuality (FActScore)\n  & Factuality degradation rate across iterations\n  & 7--9B (3 open models)\n  & 100\n  & Factual accuracy decays progressively; rate governed by source--bridge linguistic distance \\\\\n\\addlinespace\n\\citet{markovian_chains}\n  & Rephrasing + round-trip translation\n  & String recurrence time, distinct-output count, similarity plateau (METEOR), KL contraction\n  & Convergence \\& diversity dynamics of inference-time Markov chains\n  & 7--8B (3 open + GPT-4o-mini)\n  & 50\n  & Greedy decoding collapses to 1--4 recurrent outputs; sampling sustains 10--20\\,+ realizations \\\\\n\\addlinespace\n\\textbf{Ours}\n  & Open-ended continuation (no meaning-preserving constraint)\n  & Token-level NLL statistics (location, scale, shape) + lexical diversity\n  & Depth classification + DDR decomposition into generator \\& depth effects\n  & 0.6--8B (8 models)\n  & 5\n  & NLL location drifts monotonically with depth while shape remains invariant---enabling per-generator depth profiling \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\end{table*}\n\n\\paragraph{Finding-level connections.}\n\\citet{broken_telephone}'s factuality degradation under back-translation chains parallels DDR's location drift: both document monotonic loss of input-specific information with iteration depth, though the former measures semantic content loss while DDR captures distributional compression in the NLL feature space.\nTheir finding that degradation rate scales with source--bridge linguistic distance mirrors our observation that elaboration prompts attenuate DDR (Appendix~\\ref{app:prompt-type}): tighter semantic constraints slow both factuality decay and distributional drift.\n\n\\citet{markovian_chains}'s convergence dynamics under greedy decoding---collapse to 1--4 recurrent outputs within 50 steps---aligns with our greedy-decoding experiment where the DDR partition persists: shape features remain near-stationary (mean $|d|{=}0.05$) while location drift is maintained (mean $d{=}0.94$; Appendix~\\ref{app:decoding-strategy}), confirming that DDR is an empirical regularity of autoregressive generation independent of sampling stochasticity.\nTheir KL contraction analysis complements DDR: where they quantify \\emph{how fast} the output distribution contracts, DDR decomposes \\emph{what} contracts (location) from what is preserved (shape).\n",

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01HBYkZoAVh2urx72W5v81gn`</summary>

```text
1	\section{Results}
2	\label{sec:results}
3	
4	\subsection{Depth Classification Performance}
5	\label{sec:results-classification}
6	
7	The 7-feature ensemble achieves 3-class LOGO F1\,=\,0.762 (95\% CI [0.758, 0.766]; $N$\,=\,37{,}058) across eight generators from five families (Table~\ref{tab:logo-results}), with cross-generator F1 spread of 9.1\,pp capturing generator selection uncertainty.
8	This 3-class metric is inflated by shared gen-0 seeds across generators (per-class gen-0 F1\,$\approx$\,0.94; \S\ref{sec:generation}); we therefore report \textbf{leak-free binary F1} (gen-1 vs.\ gen-2+)\,=\,0.694 (CI [0.688, 0.700]) as the primary metric throughout, excluding gen-0 entirely.
9	Per-generator binary F1 ranges from 0.633 (Mistral-7B) to 0.724 (Pythia-2.8B; SD\,=\,0.028), with the primary difficulty concentrated at the gen-1\,/\,gen-2+ boundary (Appendix~\ref{app:confusion}).
10	CIs reflect bootstrap sampling variability; the cross-generator range (9.1\,pp) captures the distinct and larger uncertainty of generator selection.
11	
12	\paragraph{Cross-model generalization.}
13	Cross-model chains (randomly selected generator per step) yield binary F1\,=\,0.830 (95\% CI [.818, .841]; Appendix~\ref{app:cross-model-detail}) under 5-generator LOGO.\footnote{Pythia-2.8B/1B, OLMo-2-1B, Qwen3-0.6B/1.7B; generator-level LOGO with bootstrap CIs ($n$\,=\,1{,}000). Not directly comparable to 8-generator LOGO due to differing subsets.}
14	Single-generator transfer (Appendix~\ref{app:transfer}) confirms the signal is not a mixed-chain artifact (mean transfer F1\,=\,0.810; LOFO\,=\,0.686); the cross-model advantage is only 2\,pp; ANOVA variance decomposition confirms generator identity contributes ${<}1$\% of feature variance ($\eta^2_{\text{gen}} \leq 0.009$; Appendix~\ref{app:cross-model-detail}).
15	A scale effect emerges: Pythia achieves the highest F1 (0.718--0.724) while larger models degrade (Mistral-7B: 0.633), partly reflecting same-family affinity with the Pythia-410M reference (cross-reference spread $\leq$0.013; \S\ref{sec:results-differential}); cross-domain signal persists with variation (Appendix~\ref{app:domain}).
16	An end-to-end QLoRA classifier achieves 3-class F1\,=\,0.905 under 6-generator LOGO ($+$11.6\,pp vs.\ XGBoost 0.789; Appendix~\ref{app:qlora}), confirming richer token-level representations capture additional depth signal, albeit requiring per-generator fine-tuning; on instruct data, QLoRA recovers to 0.89--0.91 (Appendix~\ref{app:instruct}), establishing the decomposition, not the classifier, as DDR's primary contribution.
17	The 11.6\,pp gap likely reflects richer input representations; the relative contributions of representation, optimization, and capacity remain undisentangled.
18	
19	\paragraph{Length confound control.}
20	Length-matched subsampling yields binary F1\,=\,0.724 (vs.\ 0.770 unmatched); a random-subsample control verifies the drop reflects deconfounding, not sample-size reduction (Appendix~\ref{app:length}).
21	
22	\paragraph{Seed-sharing control.}
23	Seed-level demeaning (F1\,=\,0.701) and an independent-seed experiment with disjoint seed pools (F1\,=\,0.680; Appendix~\ref{app:seed-control}) both verify that the classifier captures generative degradation, not seed-specific content.
24	
25	\begin{table}[t]
26	\centering
27	\footnotesize
28	\caption{8-generator LOGO macro-F1 for the 7-feature ensemble (bootstrap 95\% CIs, $n$\,=\,1000). Binary F1 (gen-1 vs.\ gen-2+) is the primary metric; it excludes gen-0 entirely and is unaffected by seed sharing. Cross-generator binary F1: SD\,=\,0.028, IQR\,=\,[0.692, 0.714]. $^{\dagger}$3-class F1 carries upward bias because gen-0 seeds are shared across generators (\S\ref{sec:generation}); independent-seed controls confirm the depth signal (F1\,=\,0.680; Appendix~\ref{app:seed-control}).}
29	\label{tab:logo-results}
30	\resizebox{\columnwidth}{!}{%
31	\begin{tabular}{@{}llcccc@{}}

37	Qwen3-1.7B     & Qwen    & 1.7B & 0.709 & [.693, .726] & 0.772 \\

56	\caption{Feature trajectories across generation depth ($y$-axis: $z$-scored feature values). \textbf{Top row (drifting)}: entropy mean and hapax ratio decline monotonically across all eight generators ($|\rho| > 0.59$). \textbf{Bottom row (stationary)}: entropy max and tail decay remain flat ($|\rho| < 0.07$). Colored lines: individual generators; diamonds: cross-generator mean (blue for drifting, amber for stationary). This dichotomy defines the DDR.}

      "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_3/projects/gen-depth-fingerprint/docs/paper/results.tex",
      "content": "\\section{Results}\n\\label{sec:results}\n\n\\subsection{Depth Classification Performance}\n\\label{sec:results-classification}\n\nThe 7-feature ensemble achieves 3-class LOGO F1\\,=\\,0.762 (95\\% CI [0.758, 0.766]; $N$\\,=\\,37{,}058) across eight generators from five families (Table~\\ref{tab:logo-results}), with cross-generator F1 spread of 9.1\\,pp capturing generator selection uncertainty.\nThis 3-class metric is inflated by shared gen-0 seeds across generators (per-class gen-0 F1\\,$\\approx$\\,0.94; \\S\\ref{sec:generation}); we therefore report \\textbf{leak-free binary F1} (gen-1 vs.\\ gen-2+)\\,=\\,0.694 (CI [0.688, 0.700]) as the primary metric throughout, excluding gen-0 entirely.\nPer-generator binary F1 ranges from 0.633 (Mistral-7B) to 0.724 (Pythia-2.8B; SD\\,=\\,0.028), with the primary difficulty concentrated at the gen-1\\,/\\,gen-2+ boundary (Appendix~\\ref{app:confusion}).\nCIs reflect bootstrap sampling variability; the cross-generator range (9.1\\,pp) captures the distinct and larger uncertainty of generator selection.\n\n\\paragraph{Cross-model generalization.}\nCross-model chains (randomly selected generator per step) yield binary F1\\,=\\,0.830 (95\\% CI [.818, .841]; Appendix~\\ref{app:cross-model-detail}) under 5-generator LOGO.\\footnote{Pythia-2.8B/1B, OLMo-2-1B, Qwen3-0.6B/1.7B; generator-level LOGO with bootstrap CIs ($n$\\,=\\,1{,}000). Not directly comparable to 8-generator LOGO due to differing subsets.}\nSingle-generator transfer (Appendix~\\ref{app:transfer}) confirms the signal is not a mixed-chain artifact (mean transfer F1\\,=\\,0.810; LOFO\\,=\\,0.686); the cross-model advantage is only 2\\,pp; ANOVA variance decomposition confirms generator identity contributes ${<}1$\\% of feature variance ($\\eta^2_{\\text{gen}} \\leq 0.009$; Appendix~\\ref{app:cross-model-detail}).\nA scale effect emerges: Pythia achieves the highest F1 (0.718--0.724) while larger models degrade (Mistral-7B: 0.633), partly reflecting same-family affinity with the Pythia-410M reference (cross-reference spread $\\leq$0.013; \\S\\ref{sec:results-differential}); cross-domain signal persists with variation (Appendix~\\ref{app:domain}).\nAn end-to-end QLoRA classifier achieves 3-class F1\\,=\\,0.905 under 6-generator LOGO ($+$11.6\\,pp vs.\\ XGBoost 0.789; Appendix~\\ref{app:qlora}), confirming richer token-level representations capture additional depth signal, albeit requiring per-generator fine-tuning; on instruct data, QLoRA recovers to 0.89--0.91 (Appendix~\\ref{app:instruct}), establishing the decomposition, not the classifier, as DDR's primary contribution.\nThe 11.6\\,pp gap likely reflects richer input representations; the relative contributions of representation, optimization, and capacity remain undisentangled.\n\n\\paragraph{Length confound control.}\nLength-matched subsampling yields binary F1\\,=\\,0.724 (vs.\\ 0.770 unmatched); a random-subsample control verifies the drop reflects deconfounding, not sample-size reduction (Appendix~\\ref{app:length}).\n\n\\paragraph{Seed-sharing control.}\nSeed-level demeaning (F1\\,=\\,0.701) and an independent-seed experiment with disjoint seed pools (F1\\,=\\,0.680; Appendix~\\ref{app:seed-control}) both verify that the classifier captures generative degradation, not seed-specific content.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\footnotesize\n\\caption{8-generator LOGO macro-F1 for the 7-feature ensemble (bootstrap 95\\% CIs, $n$\\,=\\,1000). Binary F1 (gen-1 vs.\\ gen-2+) is the primary metric; it excludes gen-0 entirely and is unaffected by seed sharing. Cross-generator binary F1: SD\\,=\\,0.028, IQR\\,=\\,[0.692, 0.714]. $^{\\dagger}$3-class F1 carries upward bias because gen-0 seeds are shared across generators (\\S\\ref{sec:generation}); independent-seed controls confirm the depth signal (F1\\,=\\,0.680; Appendix~\\ref{app:seed-control}).}\n\\label{tab:logo-results}\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}llcccc@{}}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Model} & \\textbf{Family} & \\textbf{Params} & \\textbf{Bin F1} & \\textbf{95\\% CI} & \\textbf{3-class F1}$^{\\dagger}$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nPythia-2.8B     & Pythia  & 2.8B & \\textbf{0.724} & [.707, .742] & 0.798 \\\\\nPythia-1B       & Pythia  & 1.0B & 0.718 & [.700, .735] & \\textbf{0.806} \\\\\nQwen3-1.7B     & Qwen    & 1.7B & 0.709 & [.693, .726] & 0.772 \\\\\nLlama-3.2-3B   & Llama   & 3.2B & 0.697 & [.680, .713] & 0.775 \\\\\nOLMo-2-1B      & OLMo    & 1.5B & 0.695 & [.679, .711] & 0.781 \\\\\nQwen3-8B       & Qwen    & 8.2B & 0.692 & [.674, .708] & 0.714 \\\\\nQwen3-0.6B     & Qwen    & 0.6B & 0.691 & [.673, .709] & 0.780 \\\\\nMistral-7B     & Mistral & 7.0B & 0.633 & [.617, .648] & 0.682 \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{3}{l}{\\textbf{Average (8-gen LOGO)}} & \\textbf{0.694} & [.688, .700] & \\textbf{0.762} \\\\\n\\multicolumn{3}{l}{\\textbf{LOFO (family-level)}} & 0.686 & --- & --- \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\subsection{Differential Depth Response}\n\\label{sec:results-differential}\n\n\\begin{figure*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\textwidth]{fig_ddr_trajectory.pdf}\n\\caption{Feature trajectories across generation depth ($y$-axis: $z$-scored feature values). \\textbf{Top row (drifting)}: entropy mean and hapax ratio decline monotonically across all eight generators ($|\\rho| > 0.59$). \\textbf{Bottom row (stationary)}: entropy max and tail decay remain flat ($|\\rho| < 0.07$). Colored lines: individual generators; diamonds: cross-generator mean (blue for drifting, amber for stationary). This dichotomy defines the DDR.}\n\\label{fig:ddr-trajectory}\n\\end{figure*}\n\n\\paragraph{Formal definition.}",
      "numLines": 60,
      "startLine": 1,
      "totalLines": 142
    }
  },

          " A feature is classified as \\emph{drifting} if Cohen's $d > 0.50$ between adjacent depths, or \\emph{stationary} if $d < 0.15$; these thresholds are reference anchors, not definitive boundaries---the two-cluster structure is robust across $\\varepsilon \\in [0.10, 0.75]$, supported by unsupervised $k$-means (silhouette\\,=\\,0.660, $p{=}0.030$), exhaustive enumeration of all 35 splits (this partition ranks first), and independent replication in all eight generators (Appendix~\\ref{app:ddr-validation}); crucially, each generator's data is fully independent, making per-generator split analysis an 8-fold independent validation of the partition rather than same-data circularity."

118	\textbf{Method} & \textbf{Feat.} & \textbf{3-class F1} & \textbf{Bin F1} & \textbf{ms/text} \\

120	4-det.\ + surface     & 6 & \textbf{0.795} & \textbf{0.708} & 22.8 \\
121	4-detector ens.\      & 4 & 0.785 & 0.700 & 22.8 \\
122	7-feature (ours)      & 7 & 0.774 & 0.705 & \textbf{9.7} \\
123	\midrule

125	FastDetectGPT         & 1 & 0.660 & 0.569 & --- \\
126	LD-Score              & 1 & 0.630 & 0.675 & --- \\

132	\begin{figure}[t]
133	\centering
134	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{fig3_baseline_comparison.pdf}
135	\caption{LOGO binary F1 vs.\ inference cost in ms/text (8-gen pooled). The 7-feature ensemble (one 410M reference model) matches multi-detector ensembles (two models, 1.4B total) at ${\sim}2.4\times$ lower cost; individual detectors vary widely when repurposed for depth. Inference costs measured on a single NVIDIA RTX 5090.}
136	\label{fig:baseline-ci}
137	\end{figure}
138	
139	The 7-feature ensemble matches the 4-detector+surface ensemble (binary F1: 0.705 vs.\ 0.708; Figure~\ref{fig:baseline-ci}) at ${\sim}2.4\times$ lower cost, surpassing all individual detectors by 3.0--16.6\,pp.
140	DDR's contribution is the decomposition into location-sensitive and shape-stable components (\S\ref{sec:discussion}), with classification performance comparable to baseline ensembles rather than a performance advance.
141	Ordinal-specific methods (CORAL, Frank-Hall) yield comparable F1 under this setup (Appendix~\ref{app:classifier}), suggesting limited advantage from ranking-aware losses at 3-class resolution.
142

_source line 6624 · 2026-05-22T16:29:30.145Z_

This session is being continued from a previous conversation that ran out of context. The summary below covers the earlier portion of the conversation.

Summary:
1. Primary Request and Intent:
   User (陈卓凡) is finalizing "Depth Profiling of Recursive LLM Generation Chains" for EMNLP 2026 ARR (deadline May 25, 2026). Four sequential layout/visual requests:
   - Fix Table 1 horizontal rules being too long + page 8 right column stretching
   - Add visual figures to first 4 pages (was all text)
   - Fix main text exceeding 8 pages by compressing Discussion; fix whitespace on appendix pages 25 and 36
   - Move Figures 2 and 3 earlier so first 6 pages have more than just 1 figure

2. Key Technical Concepts:
   - EMNLP 2026 ARR: 8-page main text limit (Limitations/Ethics/References unlimited)
   - LaTeX two-column layout: `figure*` goes to top of NEXT page; `figure[t]` floats to top of current/next column
   - `\enlargethispage` stretches BOTH columns in two-column layout
   - `\flushbottom` (default) stretches vertical glue to fill columns; `\raggedbottom` collects whitespace at bottom
   - `@{}` in tabular removes outer column padding, shortening booktabs rules
   - `\tabcolsep` controls inter-column spacing in tabular
   - DDR (Differential Depth Response): location features drift with depth, shape features stable
   - Paper has 37 total pages (8 main + limitations/ethics/references + 28 appendix pages)

3. Files and Code Sections:

   - **docs/paper/method.tex** — Table 1 feature table
     - Changed `\begin{tabular}{llp{3.8cm}}` to `\begin{tabular}{@{}llp{3.0cm}@{}}`
     - Added `\setlength{\tabcolsep}{4pt}` before `\begin{tabular}`
     - Shortens horizontal rules by ~20%

   - **docs/paper/conclusion.tex** — Page 8 right column fix
     - Removed `\enlargethispage{4\baselineskip}` (was re-added by Paper Agent after earlier removal)
     - Current content:
       ```latex
       \vspace{-3mm}
       \section{Conclusion}
       \label{sec:conclusion}
       \vspace{-2mm}
       
       \looseness=-1 Recursive generation produces a decomposable depth response---location features drift while shape is approximately preserved---enabling cross-generator depth classification (F1\,=\,0.694--0.762); extending to instruct models remains open.
       ```

   - **docs/paper/introduction.tex** — Figure 1 (DDR strip plot) moved here
     - Inserted `\begin{figure}[t!]...\end{figure}` block for `fig:feature-drift` after line 10 (where it's referenced as `Figure~\ref{fig:feature-drift}`)
     - Uses `fig1_differential_response.pdf`, single-column figure
     - Now appears on page 2 left column

   - **docs/paper/results.tex** — Major figure reorganization
     - Figure 2 (`fig:ddr-trajectory`, `figure*[t]`, full-width trajectory plot) moved from subsection 4.2 (original line 53) to very beginning of Results section (before subsection 4.1)
     - Figure 3 (`fig:baseline-ci`, `figure[t]`, baseline comparison) moved from subsection 4.3 (original line 132) to beginning of subsection 4.2
     - Both figures removed from original locations and re-inserted at new positions
     - Cross-references via `\ref` still work automatically

   - **docs/paper/discussion.tex** — Compressed to fit 8-page limit
     - Scope paragraph compressed: removed redundant phrases ("without per-generator calibration", "(6/7 features show $\rho$ reversal)", human-edit degradation detail)
     - Changed end `\vspace{-1mm}` to `\vspace{-2mm}`
     - Current Discussion is 16 lines (was 17)
     - Key compression in lines 15-16:
       ```latex
       \looseness=-1 DDR is mechanistically distinct from model collapse \cite{curse_of_recursion,strong_model_collapse}---retraining erodes tail coverage via weight updates, whereas inference-chain drift shifts only location---and complements \citeauthor{broken_telephone}'s factuality degradation and \citeauthor{markovian_chains}'s convergence dynamics by adding location/shape decomposition; all three observe saturation at ${\sim}$3--5 iterations (Appendix~\ref{app:iterative-comparison}).
       Instruction tuning compresses DDR, though QLoRA recovers F1\,=\,0.89--0.91 (Appendix~\ref{app:instruct}); cross-domain signal persists at F1\,=\,0.74--0.75 (Appendix~\ref{app:domain}).
       ```

   - **docs/paper/main.tex** — Added `\raggedbottom` for appendix
     - Inserted `\raggedbottom` after `\appendix` (line 46) to fix page 25/36 whitespace
     ```latex
     \appendix
     \raggedbottom
     \renewcommand{\thesection}{\alphalph{\value{section}}}
     ```

   - **docs/paper/appendix_feature_distributions.tex** — Removed `\begingroup/\endgroup` wrapper
     - Removed `\begingroup`, `\setlength{\parskip}{2pt plus 1pt}`, and `\endgroup`
     - Kept figure definitions and text unchanged

   - **docs/paper/appendix_iterative_comparison.tex** — Table placement reverted
     - Tried `[!t]` but reverted back to `[!b]` since `table*` in two-column always goes to next page

4. Errors and fixes:

        "content": "Table~\\ref{tab:iterative-comparison} compares this work with two concurrent studies on iterative LLM generation.\nAll three examine how repeated inference transforms text, but differ in chain operator, analytic lens, and downstream goal.\nOur continuation-chain setting permits richer depth signal and enables the location/shape decomposition that defines DDR.\n\n\\begin{table*}[!b]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Comparison with concurrent iterative-generation studies. All three examine how repeated LLM inference transforms text, but differ in chain operator, analytic lens, and downstream goal.}\n\\label{tab:iterative-comparison}\n\\resizebox{\\textwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}l p{2.2cm} p{2.4cm} p{2.4cm} p{2.0cm} c p{3.4cm}@{}}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Study} & \\textbf{Chain Type} & \\textbf{Feature Space} & \\textbf{Analysis Goal} & \\textbf{Generator Scale} & \\textbf{Steps} & \\textbf{Core Insight} \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\citet{broken_telephone}\n  & Back-translation (6 bridge langs); rephrasing (ablation)\n  & Surface overlap (BLEU, ROUGE, METEOR, ChrF, BERTScore) + atomic factuality (FActScore)\n  & Factuality degradation rate across iterations\n  & 7--9B (3 open models)\n  & 100\n  & Factual accuracy decays progressively; rate governed by source--bridge linguistic distance \\\\\n\\addlinespace\n\\citet{markovian_chains}\n  & Rephrasing + round-trip translation\n  & String recurrence time, distinct-output count, similarity plateau (METEOR), KL contraction\n  & Convergence \\& diversity dynamics of inference-time Markov chains\n  & 7--8B (3 open + GPT-4o-mini)\n  & 50\n  & Greedy decoding collapses to 1--4 recurrent outputs; sampling sustains 10--20\\,+ realizations \\\\\n\\addlinespace\n\\textbf{Ours}\n  & Open-ended continuation (no meaning-preserving constraint)\n  & Token-level NLL statistics (location, scale, shape) + lexical diversity\n  & Depth classification + DDR decomposition into generator \\& depth effects\n  & 0.6--8B (8 models)\n  & 5\n  & NLL location drifts monotonically with depth while shape remains invariant---enabling per-generator depth profiling \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\end{table*}\n\n\\paragraph{Finding-level connections.}\n\\citet{broken_telephone}'s factuality degradation under back-translation chains parallels DDR's location drift: both document monotonic loss of input-specific information with iteration depth, though the former measures semantic content loss while DDR captures distributional compression in the NLL feature space.\nTheir finding that degradation rate scales with source--bridge linguistic distance mirrors our observation that elaboration prompts attenuate DDR (Appendix~\\ref{app:prompt-type}): tighter semantic constraints slow both factuality decay and distributional drift.\n\n\\citet{markovian_chains}'s convergence dynamics under greedy decoding---collapse to 1--4 recurrent outputs within 50 steps---aligns with our greedy-decoding experiment where the DDR partition persists: shape features remain near-stationary (mean $|d|{=}0.05$) while location drift is maintained (mean $d{=}0.94$; Appendix~\\ref{app:decoding-strategy}), confirming that DDR is an empirical regularity of autoregressive generation independent of sampling stochasticity.\nTheir KL contraction analysis complements DDR: where they quantify \\emph{how fast} the output distribution contracts, DDR decomposes \\emph{what} contracts (location) from what is preserved (shape).\n",
        "numLines": 48,

        "totalLines": 48
      }

        "content": "\\subsection*{DDR Trend Lines}\n\nFigure~\\ref{fig:ddr-trends} shows the per-feature depth trend (mean across all samples at each depth) with individual generator traces overlaid.\nThe consistent downward trajectories for drifting features across all generators confirm that DDR is a universal property of recursive generation.\nShape features exhibit flat trajectories regardless of generator, with near-zero $R^2$ for the linear fit.\n$R^2$ values are computed from ordinary least-squares regression on depth-level means (five points per generator, one per depth 0--4), not per-sample regression; the annotated Spearman $\\rho$ provides the complementary per-sample monotonicity measure.\n\n\\begin{figure}[H]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig_appendix_ddr_trends.pdf}\n\\caption{Per-feature depth trends. Bold line: pooled mean ($\\pm$1.96\\,SE shading) across all generators, colored by DDR zone (blue: drifting, wine: intermediate, amber: near-stationary). Thin traces: individual generators. Spearman $\\rho$ and linear $R^2$ are annotated per panel.}\n\\label{fig:ddr-trends}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\subsection*{UMAP Embedding}\n\nFigure~\\ref{fig:umap} shows a 2D UMAP projection of the 7-feature space (7{,}500 stratified samples, StandardScaler normalization, $k$\\,=\\,30 neighbors, min\\_dist\\,=\\,0.3).\nPanel~(a) reveals a depth gradient from gen-0 through gen-4, with progressive overlap at deeper generations consistent with the convergence that motivates the gen-2+ class collapse.\nPanel~(b) shows the 3-class view: gen-0 forms a separable cluster, while gen-1 and gen-2+ overlap substantially, explaining the classification difficulty at this boundary.\n\n\\begin{figure}[H]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth, trim=0 0 356pt 0, clip]{fig_appendix_umap.pdf}\\\\[-2pt]\n{\\small (a) Colored by raw generation depth (0--4)}\\\\[6pt]\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth, trim=356pt 0 0 0, clip]{fig_appendix_umap.pdf}\\\\[-2pt]\n{\\small (b) Colored by 3-class label}\n\\caption{UMAP 2D projection of the 7-feature space ($N$\\,=\\,7{,}500 stratified sample). (a) A gradient structure emerges, with deeper depths converging. (b) Gen-0 separates clearly; gen-1 and gen-2+ overlap, consistent with the high false-negative rate at the gen-1/gen-2+ boundary.}\n\\label{fig:umap}\n\\end{figure}\n",

        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 30
      }

        "content": "\\vspace{-2mm}\n\\section{Discussion}\n\\label{sec:discussion}\n\\vspace{-2mm}\n\n%% Mechanism — why DDR exists\n\\looseness=-1 We hypothesize that DDR reflects an empirical regularity of base-model autoregressive generation: recursive inputs compress the token-level NLL distribution monotonically (Appendix~\\ref{app:nll-distribution}), producing location drift independent of the token-selection mechanism.\nControlled ablations support this: greedy decoding preserves the full partition (Appendix~\\ref{app:decoding-strategy}), temperature modulates drift magnitude while preserving the partition (Appendix~\\ref{app:sampling-sensitivity}), and semantic-constraint attenuation confirms predictions (Appendix~\\ref{app:prompt-type}).\nANOVA quantifies the asymmetry: $\\eta^2_{\\text{depth}}{=}0.34$--$0.37$ for location vs.\\ ${<}0.01$ for shape (Appendix~\\ref{app:variance-decomposition}).\n\n%% Implications — what DDR enables\n\\looseness=-1 Depth constitutes a new analytical dimension for corpus auditing; although per-instance gen-2+ recall is limited (${\\sim}$27\\%), batch contamination estimation reaches ${\\pm}3$--$4$\\% at $n{=}1{,}000$ (Appendix~\\ref{app:error-analysis}), sufficient for auditing where the goal is contamination proportion rather than per-text judgment.\n\n%% Scope \\& Connections — where DDR works and fails\n\\looseness=-1 DDR is mechanistically distinct from model collapse \\cite{curse_of_recursion,strong_model_collapse}---retraining erodes tail coverage via weight updates, whereas inference-chain drift shifts only location---and complements \\citeauthor{broken_telephone}'s factuality degradation and \\citeauthor{markovian_chains}'s convergence dynamics by adding location/shape decomposition; all three observe saturation at ${\\sim}$3--5 iterations (Appendix~\\ref{app:iterative-comparison}).\nInstruction tuning compresses DDR, though QLoRA recovers F1\\,=\\,0.89--0.91 (Appendix~\\ref{app:instruct}); cross-domain signal persists at F1\\,=\\,0.74--0.75 (Appendix~\\ref{app:domain}).\n\\vspace{-2mm}\n",
        "numLines": 18,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 18
      }

Also use for: permissions (\"allow X\", \"add permission\", \"move permission to\"), env vars (\"set X=Y\"), hook troubleshooting, or any changes to settings.json/settings.local.json files. Examples: \"allow npm commands\", \"add bq permission to global settings\", \"move permission to user settings\", \"set DEBUG=true\", \"when claude stops show X\". For simple settings like theme/model, suggest the /config command.\n- keybindings-help: Use when the user wants to customize keyboard shortcuts, rebind keys, add chord bindings, or modify ~/.claude/keybindings.json. Examples: \"rebind ctrl+s\", \"add a chord shortcut\", \"change the submit key\", \"customize keybindings\".\n- simplify: Review changed code for reuse, quality, and efficiency, then fix any issues found.\n- fewer-permission-prompts: Scan your transcripts for common read-only Bash and MCP tool calls, then add a prioritized allowlist to project .claude/settings.json to reduce permission prompts.\n- loop: Run a prompt or slash command on a recurring interval (e.g. /loop 5m /foo). Omit the interval to let the model self-pace. - When the user wants to set up a recurring task, poll for status, or run something repeatedly on an interval (e.g. \"check the deploy every 5 minutes\", \"keep running /babysit-prs\"). Do NOT invoke for one-off tasks.\n- schedule: Create, update, list, or run scheduled remote agents (routines) that execute on a cron schedule. - When the user wants to schedule a recurring remote agent, set up automated tasks, create a cron job for Claude Code, or manage their scheduled agents/routines. Also use when the user wants a one-time scheduled run (\"run this once at 3pm\", \"remind me to check X tomorrow\").\n- claude-api: Build, debug, and optimize Claude API / Anthropic SDK apps. 13	## Story Types
14	
15	**突破局限型** (4/8): eliminate prior methods, reveal hidden dimension. **化繁为简型** (3/8): complex to simple principle. **跨界迁移型** (1/8): analogy-driven (Broken Telephone). **High.**
16	
17	## Intro Patterns
18	
19	**4 paragraphs** most common (4/8). 2-paragraph for simple-method papers (Binoculars, Writing Diversity). 5-paragraph for theory papers (Strong Model Collapse, QuRating). **Hooks**: societal urgency (3/8), bold contradiction (1/8), data crisis (1/8). **Elimination ladder** (2/8): each paragraph kills a method class. **Contributions**: 3 numbered items (3/8); research questions for empirical (1/8); implicit for simplicity papers (2/8). **High.**
20	
21	## Named-Problem Technique
22	
23	"Capybara problem" (Binoculars), "Strong" naming (Strong Model Collapse), "Broken telephone" analogy create citeable anchors. 3/8, high payoff. **Medium.**
24	
25	## Figure 1
26	
27	**Method overview** (4/8): pipeline diagram. **Motivation evidence** (2/8): distribution plot showing the signal. **Teaser result** (2/8): headline performance upfront. Choose teaser when result IS the surprise. **High.**
28	
29	## Result Visualization
30	
31	- **Log-scale FPR ROC** mandatory for detection (2/8). Standard AUC hides low-FPR weakness.
32	- **Qualitative text table before metrics** (3/8): degradation examples as Table 1 (Curious Decline URLs, Broken Telephone lorry->car, Binoculars capybara). Makes metrics visceral.
33	- **Line plots** for iterative (5/8). **Bar charts** for cross-method (4/8). **Theory-experiment overlays** for theory papers.
34	- **High.**
35	
36	## Table Conventions
37	
38	- Rows=methods, cols=datasets x metrics (4/8). Bold best (3/8). Symbolic markers for density.
39	- Single-run in main; std/CI in appendix (5/8).
40	- **Main:appendix split** standard: 2-3 main + extensive appendix (DNA-GPT 3+13). Core claims in main.
41	- Theory papers skip tables entirely (Strong Model Collapse: 0 tables).
42	- 3-8 baselines in main table (median 5). **High.**
43	
44	## Figure Narrative Arc
45	
46	**Standard**: method->evidence->results->robustness->ablation (4/8). **Inverted**: result-first when surprise (Binoculars). **Deepening**: what->where->why (Writing Diversity). **Theory flow**: claim->framework->validate->extend. **High.**
47	
48	## Ablation Presentation
49	
50	- Full numbers dominate (6/8); checkmark grid rare. **Embedded in main results** (3/8): mitigation lines alongside decline curves saves space.
51	- **Compute equivalence** framing more compelling than raw deltas (QuRating). **Medium.**
52

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01LpBj876jwSzQDApDRK82Pz`</summary>

```text
1	\section{Introduction}
2	\label{sec:introduction}
3	
4	An estimated 7--17\% of recent scientific publications already contain substantial LLM-modified content \cite{mapping_llm_science,monitoring_ai_review}. As these outputs re-enter training corpora \cite{dolma,fineweb} and seed further generation, text accumulates at varying \emph{generation depths}; each successive cycle reshapes distributional properties in ways that matter for data provenance, corpus auditing, and contamination monitoring in base-model pretraining corpora \cite{ccnet,dsir} as well as model collapse analysis \cite{curse_of_recursion,strong_model_collapse}. Understanding \emph{how} recursive generation alters text distributions---and which properties change versus which are preserved---is therefore a prerequisite for principled corpus curation, yet current methods address only binary human-or-machine detection \cite{detectgpt,fast_detectgpt,binoculars,ld_score}.
5	Base models provide the cleanest substrate to isolate depth effects from alignment-induced changes.
6	We find that recursive generation produces a decomposable depth profile: distributional \emph{location} drifts with depth while \emph{shape} remains stable across generators, an asymmetry sufficient for cross-generator depth prediction.
7	
8	Distributional compression under recursive generation is well-documented \cite{curious_decline,broken_telephone,markovian_chains}, yet existing analyses characterize \emph{that} and \emph{why} convergence occurs without decomposing what drifts from what is preserved.
9	We introduce \emph{depth profiling}, classifying text by ordinal generation depth (gen-0\,/\,gen-1\,/\,gen-2+), and decompose this compression into structurally distinct components: across eight base-model generators spanning five architectural families (0.6B--8B), the NLL distribution's central tendency (mean entropy (reference-model NLL), lexical diversity) drifts monotonically with depth while its shape (entropy extremes, tail structure) remains an order of magnitude more stable ($d{=}0.76$--$1.17$ vs.\ $d < 0.10$ for three near-stationary features; entropy std is intermediate ($d{=}0.55$); boundary features show additional nuance; convergent effect-size measures confirm this partition).
10	We term this location/shape asymmetry the \emph{differential depth response} (DDR; Figure~\ref{fig:feature-drift}), an empirical regularity of base-model autoregressive generation that is mechanistically distinct from model collapse \cite{curse_of_recursion}, which erodes tail coverage via weight updates; DDR instead analyzes fixed-parameter inference chains.
11	The stationary shape component provides the cross-generator reference needed for depth prediction without per-generator calibration.
12	
13	\begin{figure}[t!]
14	\centering
15	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{fig1_differential_response.pdf}
16	\caption{Differential depth response (DDR). Diamonds: cross-generator means; dots: per-generator values. Drifting features (blue; $|\rho| > 0.60$, $d{=}0.76$--$1.17$) show strong monotonic depth sensitivity; non-drifting features remain stable. Entropy std (wine; $d{=}0.55$) is an intermediate member; near-stationary features (amber; $d \leq 0.10$) show negligible drift. Entropy min ($|\rho|{=}0.40$ pooled; gen-0/gen-1 sensitive) and tail decay (TOST 4/8) are boundary features within this group. This location/shape asymmetry---the DDR---enables depth classification without per-generator calibration. Marker shapes encode feature identity for grayscale accessibility.}
17	\label{fig:feature-drift}
18	\end{figure}
19	We focus on base (pretrained) models; instruction tuning degrades depth signal (Appendix~\ref{app:instruct}).
20	
21	As a proof of concept, a lightweight 7-feature ensemble informed by DDR achieves leak-free binary F1\,=\,0.694 under leave-one-generator-out evaluation, matching multi-detector ensembles at ${\sim}2.4\times$ lower cost; an end-to-end QLoRA classifier reaches 3-class F1\,=\,0.905 (6-gen LOGO; Appendix~\ref{app:qlora}), confirming that the depth signal extends well beyond handcrafted features.
22	
23	Our contributions:
24	\begin{enumerate}[nosep,leftmargin=*]
25	    \item \textbf{Empirical decomposition (DDR).} Across eight generators from five families, we decompose inference-chain drift into a location component that shifts monotonically ($d{=}0.76$--$1.17$) and a shape component that remains near-stationary ($d < 0.10$ for three near-stationary features). Where prior work documents \emph{what converges} \cite{curious_decline,markovian_chains}, DDR identifies \emph{what is approximately preserved} and enables depth detection without per-generator calibration. DDR is a descriptive empirical finding; the classification value lies in this decomposition rather than the classifier itself.
26	    \item \textbf{Task formulation and evaluation suite.} We define generation depth classification as an ordinal prediction task and construct a controlled dataset of 37K texts from eight generators across five families (0.6B--8B), with leave-one-generator-out cross-validation as the primary evaluation protocol.
27	    \item \textbf{Scope characterization.} Systematic evaluation across temperature, prompt type, instruction tuning, and model scale maps the operational envelope of depth profiling, identifying key challenges in instruction-tuned models, cross-domain transfer, and perturbation robustness.
28	\end{enumerate}
29	
```

```text
1	\section{Method}
2	\label{sec:method}
3	
4	\begin{table}[t!]
5	\centering
6	\footnotesize
7	\caption{Seven distributional features. ``Entropy'' features are computed as per-token NLL under Pythia-410M (see \S\ref{sec:features}); surface features require no reference model. DDR analysis (\S\ref{sec:results-differential}) reveals that entropy mean and the two surface features (TTR, hapax ratio) drift with depth while entropy max, min, and tail decay remain near-stationary; entropy std is intermediate. All ``entropy'' features are reference-model NLL values (cross-entropy with Pythia-410M), not information-theoretic entropy; see \S\ref{sec:features}.}
8	\label{tab:features}
9	\setlength{\tabcolsep}{4pt}
10	\begin{tabular}{@{}llp{3.0cm}@{}}
11	\toprule
12	\textbf{Feature} & \textbf{Source} & \textbf{Measures} \\
13	\midrule
14	\texttt{entropy\_mean} & Ref.\ NLL & Central tendency of token surprise \\
15	\texttt{entropy\_std}  & Ref.\ NLL & Spread of token surprise \\
16	\texttt{entropy\_max}  & Ref.\ NLL & Maximum token surprise \\
17	\texttt{entropy\_min}  & Ref.\ NLL & Minimum token surprise \\
18	\texttt{tail\_decay}   & Ref.\ NLL & Log-prob decay slope (ranks 100--1K) \\
19	\midrule
20	\texttt{ttr}           & Surface & Type-token ratio \\
21	\texttt{hapax\_ratio}  & Surface & Proportion of hapax legomena \\
22	\bottomrule
23	\end{tabular}
24	\end{table}
25	
26	\subsection{Task Formulation}
27	\label{sec:task}
28	
29	Given a text $x$, we predict its \emph{generation depth} $d \in \{0, 1, 2{+}\}$: human-written ($d{=}0$), first-generation LLM output ($d{=}1$), or text produced by two or more recursive LLM generations ($d{=}2{+}$).
30	A generation chain of depth $k$ is defined recursively: $x^{(0)} \sim \mathcal{C}_0$ (human corpus), $x^{(k)} = G(x^{(k-1)})$ for $k \geq 1$, where $G$ denotes stochastic autoregressive generation.
31	Depths beyond 2 are merged into $2{+}$ because feature convergence compresses inter-class margins at deeper levels (\S\ref{sec:results-differential}).
32	
33	\subsection{Generation Chain Construction}
34	\label{sec:generation}
35	
36	We construct generation chains using eight generators spanning five model families: \textbf{Pythia} (2.8B, 1B) \cite{pythia}, \textbf{OLMo} (OLMo-2-1B) \cite{olmo}, \textbf{Qwen} (Qwen3-1.7B, Qwen3-0.6B, Qwen3-8B-Base) \cite{qwen3}, \textbf{Llama} (Llama-3.2-3B) \cite{llama3}, and \textbf{Mistral} (Mistral-7B-v0.3) \cite{mistral7b}.
37	These generators span five distinct architectures with a 13$\times$ parameter range (0.6B--8B), trained on heterogeneous corpora; Qwen contributes three models (0.6B--8B) covering the full scale range; Qwen's overrepresentation (3/8 generators) is a limitation, but LOFO holding out all three Qwen models (F1\,=\,0.686) provides the strictest family-level test.
38	
39	For each generator, we sample approximately 1{,}000 seed passages from English Wikipedia as $\mathcal{C}_0$.
40	The same pool of seed passages serves as gen-0 for all generators.
41	We use shared seeds across generators so that cross-generator comparisons control for input content and isolate the effect of the generation model from source text variation.
42	In LOGO evaluation, gen-0 samples from the held-out generator's chains also appear (with the same text) in the training generators' chains, creating a potential train--test overlap for the gen-0 class.
43	We address this with a supplementary binary evaluation excluding gen-0 entirely (\S\ref{sec:results-baselines}).
44	Each seed is iteratively fed through the generator to produce a chain of depth 4 (i.e., gen-0 through gen-4), using the prompt ``\texttt{Continue the following text: \{text\}}''.
45	We generate up to depth~4 to empirically confirm convergence: 5-class analysis shows adjacent depths 2--4 become indistinguishable (F1\,$\approx$\,0.27; Appendix~\ref{app:finer-depth}), motivating the gen-2+ class collapse.
46	All generation uses temperature $\tau{=}0.7$, nucleus sampling with $p{=}0.9$, and maximum length of 256 new tokens.
47	After filtering chain breaks and passages too short for feature extraction, 37{,}058 texts remain across all depths (Appendix~\ref{app:dataset-stats}); depths 2--4 are collapsed into $2{+}$.
48	
49	\subsection{Feature Extraction}
50	\label{sec:features}
51	
52	\paragraph{Terminology.} Throughout this paper, we refer to per-token cross-entropy loss under a fixed reference model as ``entropy'' for conciseness; these are reference-model NLL values, not true information-theoretic entropy of the generating distribution.
53	
54	We deliberately use interpretable distributional statistics so that the DDR partition remains transparent; end-to-end neural features improve classification ($+$11.6\,pp; Appendix~\ref{app:qlora}) but obscure the decomposition.
55	Leave-one-feature-out analysis (Appendix~\ref{app:lofo}) confirms that both reference-model and surface feature groups contribute independently.
56	
57	We extract seven distributional features per text (Table~\ref{tab:features}), selected to provide principled coverage of the NLL distribution---location (mean), spread (std), extremes (max, min), and tail structure (decay)---complemented by two reference-free surface diversity measures.
58	We evaluated six additional NLL statistics (median, 90th percentile, IQR, CV, skewness, kurtosis) as candidate features; all exhibit $|r| > 0.65$ with existing features and yield no classification gain when added ($\Delta$F1\,$\leq$\,$-0.011$; Appendix~\ref{app:feature-candidate}).
59	
60	\paragraph{Reference-model features.}
61	A held-out reference model (Pythia-410M, chosen to be smaller than all generators) computes per-token negative log-likelihood (NLL) for each text.
62	Although Pythia-410M shares the GPT-NeoX architecture with two of the eight generators, cross-family reference validation (Appendix~\ref{app:cross-ref}) shows F1 spread of only 0.013 across Pythia, OLMo, and Qwen references, confirming DDR is reference-model agnostic; however, all tested references are causal LMs.
63	From the resulting NLL sequence, we extract five statistics: mean, standard deviation, maximum, and minimum of the token-level NLL, plus tail\_decay---the slope of the vocabulary-level averaged log-probability distribution in ranks 100--1000, capturing the power-law regime where depth-induced compression is most detectable while avoiding high-frequency head noise and sparse extreme-tail counts.
64	
65	\paragraph{Surface features.}
66	Two features capture lexical diversity without requiring a reference model: type-token ratio (TTR) and the proportion of words appearing exactly once (hapax\_ratio).
67	
68	\paragraph{Feature complementarity.}
69	These features exhibit a \emph{differential depth response} (DDR), separating into drifting and non-drifting groups (\S\ref{sec:results-differential}; pairwise correlations in Appendix~\ref{sec:appendix-feature-correlation}).
70	TTR and hapax ratio are strongly correlated ($r = 0.97$); both are retained for marginal LOFO gains.
71	
72	\subsection{Evaluation Protocol}
73	\label{sec:evaluation}
74	
75	\paragraph{Cross-validation.}
76	We evaluate generalization via \textbf{Leave-One-Generator-Out (LOGO)} cross-validation: each fold holds out one generator entirely, so the classifier must generalize to a generator absent from training---the strictest per-model protocol for assessing deployment against previously unseen generators.
77	LOGO does not isolate family-level effects; \textbf{Leave-One-Family-Out (LOFO)} addresses this by holding out entire model families (binary F1\,=\,0.686; \S\ref{sec:results-classification}).
78	Generator subsets vary across experiments (Appendix~\ref{app:gen-subsets-section}).
79	
80	\paragraph{Classifier.}
81	We use XGBoost \cite{xgboost} throughout ($n\_\text{estimators}{=}200$, $\max\_\text{depth}{=}6$, learning rate $0.1$; fixed without tuning), with identical hyperparameters across all methods in Table~\ref{tab:baselines}.
82	Alternative classifiers (CORAL \cite{coral_ordinal}, CORN \cite{corn_ordinal}, Frank-Hall \cite{frank_hall_ordinal}) yield comparable performance (Appendix~\ref{app:classifier}); discriminative power resides in the features, not the classifier.
83	
84	\paragraph{Metrics and statistical testing.}
85	The primary metric is \textbf{macro-averaged F1} over the three depth classes, with 95\% bootstrap CIs ($n{=}1{,}000$).
86	All $p$-values are two-sided with Bonferroni correction within each family of simultaneous tests.
87	
88	\paragraph{Baselines.}
89	We compare the 7-feature ensemble against four single-score detection methods repurposed as depth predictors: Avg-LogProb, FastDetectGPT \cite{fast_detectgpt}, Binoculars \cite{binoculars}, and LD-Score \cite{ld_score}.
90	Each baseline score is used as a univariate feature in the same XGBoost framework under LOGO cross-validation to ensure a controlled comparison.
91	
92	\paragraph{Length confound control.}
93	Since TTR and hapax\_ratio are mechanically length-dependent, we verify depth signal is not a length artifact via length-matched subsampling that equalizes length distributions across depth classes (\S\ref{sec:results-classification}; Appendix~\ref{app:length}).
94

additional NLL statistics (median, 90th percentile, IQR, CV, skewness, kurtosis) as candidate features; all exhibit $|r| > 0.65$ with existing features and yield no classification gain when added ($\\Delta$F1\\,$\\leq$\\,$-0.011$; Appendix~\\ref{app:feature-candidate}).\n\n\\paragraph{Reference-model features.}\nA held-out reference model (Pythia-410M, chosen to be smaller than all generators) computes per-token negative log-likelihood (NLL) for each text.\nAlthough Pythia-410M shares the GPT-NeoX architecture with two of the eight generators, cross-family reference validation (Appendix~\\ref{app:cross-ref}) shows F1 spread of only 0.013 across Pythia, OLMo, and Qwen references, confirming DDR is reference-model agnostic; however, all tested references are causal LMs.\nFrom the resulting NLL sequence, we extract five statistics: mean, standard deviation, maximum, and minimum of the token-level NLL, plus tail\\_decay---the slope of the vocabulary-level averaged log-probability distribution in ranks 100--1000, capturing the power-law regime where depth-induced compression is most detectable while avoiding high-frequency head noise and sparse extreme-tail counts.\n\n\\paragraph{Surface features.}\nTwo features capture lexical diversity without requiring a reference model: type-token ratio (TTR) and the proportion of words appearing exactly once (hapax\\_ratio).\n\n\\paragraph{Feature complementarity.}\nThese features exhibit a \\emph{differential depth response} (DDR), separating into drifting and non-drifting groups (\\S\\ref{sec:results-differential}; pairwise correlations in Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix-feature-correlation}).\nTTR and hapax ratio are strongly correlated ($r = 0.97$); both are retained for marginal LOFO gains.\n\n\\subsection{Evaluation Protocol}\n\\label{sec:evaluation}\n\n\\paragraph{Cross-validation.}\nWe evaluate generalization via \\textbf{Leave-One-Generator-Out (LOGO)} cross-validation: each fold holds out one generator entirely, so the classifier must generalize to a generator absent from training---the strictest per-model protocol for assessing deployment against previously unseen generators.\nLOGO does not isolate family-level effects; \\textbf{Leave-One-Family-Out (LOFO)} addresses this by holding out entire model families (binary F1\\,=\\,0.686; \\S\\ref{sec:results-classification}).\nGenerator subsets vary across experiments (Appendix~\\ref{app:gen-subsets-section}).\n\n\\paragraph{Classifier.}\nWe use XGBoost \\cite{xgboost} throughout ($n\\_\\text{estimators}{=}200$, $\\max\\_\\text{depth}{=}6$, learning rate $0.1$; fixed without tuning), with identical hyperparameters across all methods in Table~\\ref{tab:baselines}.\nAlternative classifiers (CORAL \\cite{coral_ordinal}, CORN \\cite{corn_ordinal}, Frank-Hall \\cite{frank_hall_ordinal}) yield comparable performance (Appendix~\\ref{app:classifier}); discriminative power resides in the features, not the classifier.\n\n\\paragraph{Metrics and statistical testing.}\nThe primary metric is \\textbf{macro-averaged F1} over the three depth classes, with 95\\% bootstrap CIs ($n{=}1{,}000$).\nAll $p$-values are two-sided with Bonferroni correction within each family of simultaneous tests.\n\n\\paragraph{Baselines.}\nWe compare the 7-feature ensemble against four single-score detection methods repurposed as depth predictors: Avg-LogProb, FastDetectGPT \\cite{fast_detectgpt}, Binoculars \\cite{binoculars}, and LD-Score \\cite{ld_score}.\nEach baseline score is used as a univariate feature in the same XGBoost framework under LOGO cross-validation to ensure a controlled comparison.\n\n\\paragraph{Length confound control.}\nSince TTR and hapax\\_ratio are mechanically length-dependent, we verify depth signal is not a length artifact via length-matched subsampling that equalizes length distributions across depth classes (\\S\\ref{sec:results-classification}; Appendix~\\ref{app:length}).\n",
      "numLines": 94,
      "startLine": 1,
      "totalLines": 94
    }

1	\section{Results}
2	\label{sec:results}
3	
4	\begin{figure*}[t]
5	\centering
6	\includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{fig_ddr_trajectory.pdf}
7	\caption{Feature trajectories across generation depth ($y$-axis: $z$-scored feature values). \textbf{Top row (drifting)}: entropy mean and hapax ratio decline monotonically across all eight generators ($|\rho| > 0.59$). \textbf{Bottom row (stationary)}: entropy max and tail decay remain flat ($|\rho| < 0.07$). Colored lines: individual generators; diamonds: cross-generator mean (blue for drifting, amber for stationary). This dichotomy defines the DDR.}
8	\label{fig:ddr-trajectory}
9	\end{figure*}
10	
11	\subsection{Depth Classification Performance}
12	\label{sec:results-classification}
13	
14	The 7-feature ensemble achieves 3-class LOGO F1\,=\,0.762 (95\% CI [0.758, 0.766]; $N$\,=\,37{,}058) across eight generators from five families (Table~\ref{tab:logo-results}), with cross-generator F1 spread of 9.1\,pp capturing generator selection uncertainty.
15	This 3-class metric is inflated by shared gen-0 seeds across generators (per-class gen-0 F1\,$\approx$\,0.94; \S\ref{sec:generation}); we therefore report \textbf{leak-free binary F1} (gen-1 vs.\ gen-2+)\,=\,0.694 (CI [0.688, 0.700]) as the primary metric throughout, excluding gen-0 entirely.
16	Per-generator binary F1 ranges from 0.633 (Mistral-7B) to 0.724 (Pythia-2.8B; SD\,=\,0.028), with the primary difficulty concentrated at the gen-1\,/\,gen-2+ boundary (Appendix~\ref{app:confusion}).
17	CIs reflect bootstrap sampling variability; the cross-generator range (9.1\,pp) captures the distinct and larger uncertainty of generator selection.
18	
19	\paragraph{Cross-model generalization.}
20	Cross-model chains (randomly selected generator per step) yield binary F1\,=\,0.830 (95\% CI [.818, .841]; Appendix~\ref{app:cross-model-detail}) under 5-generator LOGO.\footnote{Pythia-2.8B/1B, OLMo-2-1B, Qwen3-0.6B/1.7B; generator-level LOGO with bootstrap CIs ($n$\,=\,1{,}000). Not directly comparable to 8-generator LOGO due to differing subsets.}
21	Single-generator transfer (Appendix~\ref{app:transfer}) confirms the signal is not a mixed-chain artifact (mean transfer F1\,=\,0.810; LOFO\,=\,0.686); the cross-model advantage is only 2\,pp; ANOVA variance decomposition confirms generator identity contributes ${<}1$\% of feature variance ($\eta^2_{\text{gen}} \leq 0.009$; Appendix~\ref{app:cross-model-detail}).
22	A scale effect emerges: Pythia achieves the highest F1 (0.718--0.724) while larger models degrade (Mistral-7B: 0.633), partly reflecting same-family affinity with the Pythia-410M reference (cross-reference spread $\leq$0.013; \S\ref{sec:results-differential}); cross-domain signal persists with variation (Appendix~\ref{app:domain}).
23	An end-to-end QLoRA classifier achieves 3-class F1\,=\,0.905 under 6-generator LOGO ($+$11.6\,pp vs.\ XGBoost 0.789; Appendix~\ref{app:qlora}), confirming richer token-level representations capture additional depth signal, albeit requiring per-generator fine-tuning; on instruct data, QLoRA recovers to 0.89--0.91 (Appendix~\ref{app:instruct}), establishing the decomposition, not the classifier, as DDR's primary contribution.
24	The 11.6\,pp gap likely reflects richer input representations; the relative contributions of representation, optimization, and capacity remain undisentangled.
25	
26	\paragraph{Length confound control.}
27	Length-matched subsampling yields binary F1\,=\,0.724 (vs.\ 0.770 unmatched); a random-subsample control verifies the drop reflects deconfounding, not sample-size reduction (Appendix~\ref{app:length}).
28	
29	\paragraph{Seed-sharing control.}
30	Seed-level demeaning (F1\,=\,0.701) and an independent-seed experiment with disjoint seed pools (F1\,=\,0.680; Appendix~\ref{app:seed-control}) both verify that the classifier captures generative degradation, not seed-specific content.
31

Cross-generator binary F1: SD\,=\,0.028, IQR\,=\,[0.692, 0.714]. $^{\dagger}$3-class F1 carries upward bias because gen-0 seeds are shared across generators (\S\ref{sec:generation}); independent-seed controls confirm the depth signal (F1\,=\,0.680; Appendix~\ref{app:seed-control}).}

56	
57	\subsection{Differential Depth Response}
58	\label{sec:results-differential}
59	
60	\begin{figure}[t]
61	\centering
62	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{fig3_baseline_comparison.pdf}
63	\caption{LOGO binary F1 vs.\ inference cost in ms/text (8-gen pooled). The 7-feature ensemble (one 410M reference model) matches multi-detector ensembles (two models, 1.4B total) at ${\sim}2.4\times$ lower cost; individual detectors vary widely when repurposed for depth. Inference costs measured on a single NVIDIA RTX 5090.}
64	\label{fig:baseline-ci}
65	\end{figure}
66	
67	\paragraph{Formal definition.}
68	For feature $f$ measured across depths $d \in \{0,1,2{+}\}$, we define its \emph{depth sensitivity} as $\text{DDR}(f) = |\rho(\text{depth}, f)|$, where $\rho$ is Spearman correlation over all per-text measurements.
69	A feature is classified as \emph{drifting} if Cohen's $d > 0.50$ between adjacent depths, or \emph{stationary} if $d < 0.15$; these thresholds are reference anchors, not definitive boundaries---the two-cluster structure is robust across $\varepsilon \in [0.10, 0.75]$, supported by unsupervised $k$-means (silhouette\,=\,0.660, $p{=}0.030$), exhaustive enumeration of all 35 splits (this partition ranks first), and independent replication in all eight generators (Appendix~\ref{app:ddr-validation}); crucially, each generator's data is fully independent, making per-generator split analysis an 8-fold independent validation of the partition rather than same-data circularity.
70	Under autoregressive generation, recursive inputs grow increasingly self-typical---we hypothesize this reflects the mixing property of finite-vocabulary autoregressive processes~\citep{markovian_chains}---predicting that location statistics should drift while shape statistics remain invariant (confirmed across nucleus, greedy, and top-$k$ strategies; Appendix~\ref{app:decoding-strategy}).
71	Shape features (extreme ranges, tail decay) reflect architectural constraints rather than input location, explaining their stability under recursive compression.
72	
73	The classification results above establish \emph{that} depth is distinguishable; we now ask \emph{why} by examining which features respond to recursive generation.
74	Figure~\ref{fig:ddr-trajectory} reveals a consistent dichotomy across all eight generators: central-tendency features (entropy mean (reference-model NLL; \S\ref{sec:features}), TTR, hapax ratio) decline monotonically with depth, while shape features (entropy max, tail decay, entropy min) remain flat.
75	Appendix~\ref{app:qualitative} illustrates the DDR with a concrete generation chain.
76	The primary partition is binary: $k{=}2$ clustering cleanly separates three drifting from four non-drifting features; higher $k$ degrades quality ($k{=}3$: silhouette\,=\,0.532; Appendix~\ref{app:ddr-validation}).
77	A domain-informed refinement resolves entropy std as a sole \emph{intermediate} member ($d{=}0.55$, $|\rho|{=}0.16$), distinct from the three near-stationary features ($d < 0.10$; CV shift $<$3\% vs.\ $>$15\% for drifting features).
78	Multiple effect-size measures corroborate this partition (Quantification below).
79	
80	Pooled Spearman correlations with depth corroborate the binary split (Figure~\ref{fig:feature-drift}):
81	drifting features show substantial monotonic trends (hapax ratio $\rho = -0.64$, TTR $\rho = -0.63$, entropy mean $\rho = -0.60$),
82	while non-drifting features remain near-zero (tail decay $|\rho| = 0.04$; per-depth distributions in Appendix~\ref{app:feature-distributions}).
83	The six-fold magnitude gap between drifting ($|\rho| > 0.60$) and non-drifting features ($|\rho| \leq 0.16$) defines the DDR partition.
84	Within the non-drifting group, entropy std ($|\rho| = 0.16$, $d{=}0.55$) shows a substantial level shift despite low monotonicity, distinguishing it from the three near-stationary features ($d < 0.10$); this refinement is stable across $d$ thresholds in $[0.56, 0.75]$ (Appendix~\ref{app:ddr-validation}).
85	
86	\paragraph{Quantification.}
87	Parametric and non-parametric effect-size measures converge on the split.
88	Cohen's $d$ \cite{cohen1988statistical} shows a ${\geq}7\times$ gap between drifting ($d{=}0.76$--$1.17$) and near-stationary features ($d \leq 0.08$); KS statistics, Glass's $\Delta$, Cliff's $\delta$, and CV shift all corroborate (Appendix~\ref{app:ddr-validation}), with the partition robust under heteroscedasticity (Glass's $\Delta$ gap ${\geq}9.5\times$; Appendix~\ref{app:heteroscedasticity}).
89	Four features fall cleanly into drifting or non-drifting groups.
90	Entropy min and tail decay are \emph{boundary features}: entropy min is near-stationary for gen-1 vs.\ gen-2+ ($d{=}0.016$) but sensitive at gen-0/gen-1 ($|\rho|{=}0.40$); tail decay is \emph{weakly stationary}, passing TOST for only 4/8 generators ($|d|{=}0.10$--$0.22$).
91	Entropy std ($d{=}0.55$, $|\rho|{=}0.16$) is the sole intermediate member.
92	\paragraph{Validation.}
93	Per-generator split analysis provides the strongest validation: each of the eight hold-out generators independently recovers the identical drifting/non-drifting partition (per-generator silhouette range 0.505--0.760, invariant across $\varepsilon \in [0.10, 0.75]$; Appendix~\ref{app:ddr-validation}).
94	TOST equivalence testing confirms near-stationarity for entropy min (8/8 pass) and entropy max (6/8); tail decay passes for only 4/8 ($|d|{=}0.10$--$0.22$), reflecting generator-specific tail structure variation rather than systematic drift: the effect sizes remain an order of magnitude below the drifting group ($d \geq 0.76$), and the partition assignment is unchanged.
95	Permutation testing confirms the split as non-random ($p{=}0.030$); reclassifying either boundary feature preserves the core split.
96	
97	\paragraph{Feature complementarity.}
98	The full 7-feature set (F1\,=\,0.773, 10-fold stratified CV, complementing the LOGO protocol in Table~\ref{tab:logo-results}) outperforms surface-only (0.688) and reference-only (0.713; McNemar $p \approx 0$; Appendix~\ref{app:ablation-table}).
99	The collinear drifting triplet captures ${\sim}$1 effective dimension (PCA PC1\,=\,92.3\%; Appendix~\ref{sec:appendix-feature-correlation}); group ablation confirms complementarity for depth profiling: 3-class F1 gains $+$5.3\,pp when non-drifting features are added (drifting-only 0.716, all-7 0.769), while binary detection is already saturated by location drift alone (drifting-only binary F1\,=\,0.697 vs.\ all-7\,=\,0.700; Appendix~\ref{app:ablation-table})---shape stability contributes to depth \emph{grading}, not detection.
100	TTR and hapax ratio ($r = 0.97$) are retained for marginal LOFO gains \cite{strobl_conditional_2008} (Appendix~\ref{app:lofo}).
101	
102	\paragraph{Cross-family reference models.}
103	To isolate reference-model bias, we evaluate on a 5-generator subset excluding all Pythia-family generators---a stricter protocol than 8-gen LOGO.
104	Alternative references (OLMo-2-1B, Qwen3-0.6B) achieve comparable performance (F1 spread\,=\,0.013; Table~\ref{tab:cross-ref} in Appendix~\ref{app:cross-ref}), with drift direction consistently negative.
105	Same-family proximity may inflate Pythia F1 by up to 1.3\,pp, but the DDR partition is fully preserved regardless of reference choice.
106	
107	\paragraph{Temperature and prompt-type sensitivity.}
108	All four tested generators degrade at $t{=}0.9$ ($-$9.3 to $-$26.0\,pp binary F1; Appendix~\ref{app:sampling-sensitivity}); continuation chains show the strongest drift (F1\,=\,0.781; Appendix~\ref{app:prompt-type}).
109	The DDR partition hierarchy is preserved across temperatures (Appendix~\ref{app:ddr-temp-stability}).
110	
111	
112	\subsection{Comparison with Detection Baselines}
113	\label{sec:results-baselines}
114	
115	We next ask whether existing detection methods---designed for binary real-vs-synthetic classification---incidentally capture depth structure, repurposing four detection scores as ordinal depth predictors under identical XGBoost and LOGO conditions (Table~\ref{tab:baselines}).
116	The strongest baseline is a 4-detector + surface feature ensemble (binary F1\,=\,0.708), which pools four detection scores with TTR and hapax ratio; among individual detectors, Avg-LogProb provides the most meaningful single-feature comparison.
117	
118	\begin{table}[t]
119	\centering
120	\footnotesize
121	\caption{Depth classification (8-gen LOGO, pooled evaluation; pooled F1 is slightly higher than per-generator macro F1 in Table~\ref{tab:logo-results} because larger test folds from high-performing generators contribute proportionally more). Identical XGBoost configuration. 7-feature: one 410M model (905\,MB); 4-det ensembles: two models, 1.4B params (2{,}868\,MB). Individual detection scores (N/A) share the reference-model forward pass; per-method timing is not separately profiled.}
122	\label{tab:baselines}
123	\begin{tabular}{lcccc}
124	\toprule
125	\textbf{Method} & \textbf{Feat.} & \textbf{3-class F1} & \textbf{Bin F1} & \textbf{ms/text} \\
126	\midrule
127	4-det.\ + surface     & 6 & \textbf{0.795} & \textbf{0.708} & 22.8 \\
128	4-detector ens.\      & 4 & 0.785 & 0.700 & 22.8 \\
129	7-feature (ours)      & 7 & 0.774 & 0.705 & \textbf{9.7} \\
130	\midrule
131	Avg-LogProb           & 1 & 0.707 & 0.673 & --- \\
132	FastDetectGPT         & 1 & 0.660 & 0.569 & --- \\
133	LD-Score              & 1 & 0.630 & 0.675 & --- \\
134	Binoculars            & 1 & 0.411 & 0.539 & --- \\
135	\bottomrule
136	\end{tabular}
137	\end{table}
138	
139	The 7-feature ensemble matches the 4-detector+surface ensemble (binary F1: 0.705 vs.\ 0.708; Figure~\ref{fig:baseline-ci}) at ${\sim}2.4\times$ lower cost, surpassing all individual detectors by 3.0--16.6\,pp.
140	DDR's contribution is the decomposition into location-sensitive and shape-stable components (\S\ref{sec:discussion}), with classification performance comparable to baseline ensembles rather than a performance advance.
141	Ordinal-specific methods (CORAL, Frank-Hall) yield comparable F1 under this setup (Appendix~\ref{app:classifier}), suggesting limited advantage from ranking-aware losses at 3-class resolution.
142
